# Doodlume — fresh 25-perspective critical review — Product Council

Simulated specialist perspectives from25 separately delegated AI agents, not human experts or external endorsements.

25 separately delegated simulated AI specialist perspectives (23 UI/UX and 2 game design), not real external consultants or endorsements. Ratings are provisional judgments of stated inspected scopes, not an overall score or probability of winning.

## Brief

# Independent Doodlume critical review
Frozen product revision: 0a4e3e9. No product changes during review.
User goal: a commercially convincing drawing/photo-to-playable-game product with excellent desktop UI/UX, meaningful games, genuine source-driven creation, clear creative editing, and a compelling hackathon demonstration. Mobile improvements are deferred. Review actual quality, not merely whether old bug fixes passed. Do not infer that a working feature is enjoyable, exceptional or commercially validated.

25 separate simulated specialist perspectives: 23 UI/UX and 2 game design. These are AI agents, not human experts. All are from the same model family; this is structured adversarial analysis, not independent human validation.

Round1 is blind to previous ratings and other reviewers. Do NOT open docs/POST-REPAIR-COUNCIL.md, prior council JSON/reviews, or prior score reports. Source files and supplied neutral screen evidence are allowed. For later rounds read ALL earlier rounds in this case.

Scoring: provisional 0-10 commercial-quality judgment for your assigned area. 5=usable but important weaknesses;7=solid with noticeable weaknesses;9=exceptionally refined with strong evidence;10=outstanding across the inspected relevant experience, no material evidenced shortfall, with positive supporting evidence. Not finding a bug alone does not earn10. Untested outcomes must remain explicitly unverified; use not-assessed if evidence cannot support a score. No requested score, no quota of criticisms, no score averaging. Do not give scores to satisfy the owner or moderator.

Every criticism: exact screen/control/source, reproducible observation, consequence, severity, correction and acceptance. Separate observed defect, expert judgment, hypothesis and unknown. Preserve useful strengths. Avoid fashionable redesign recommendations without evidence. Review current product; don't cite obsolete artifacts as current.

Contract: read /Users/sreenath/.codex/skills/product-council/references/data-contract.md. Seat IDs are our25 roster, replacing standard10 per explicit user request. Four rounds. Write Rn-seat.json to reviews. Include normal contract fields; assessment states score/scope; add rating={score:number|null,scope,confidence,reason} and findings=[{id,kind,severity,location,observation,impact,correction,acceptance,evidence}] if useful. Round1 >=1 proposal; later can no proposals. Later responses cite other-seat earlier IDs. Keep original authored comments intact. ~150-250 substantive words, additional structured findings okay.

No production edits, commits, model calls, external communication or reading credentials. Browser controllers use CUA only. Screenshots are evidence of appearance, not proof of behavior. Do not claim to have played unless you actually did. Root and assigned gameplay reviewers collect live evidence; other reviewers inspect pixels and code. All evidence lives in this case/evidence.json, and more fresh evidence may be added.


## Execution

Frozen revision 0a4e3e9. Twenty-five new role-specific agents performed a blind initial review without prior ratings or peer comments. All 25 completed each round before the next began. Subsequent rounds received complete preceding authored comments, with cumulative records available; each response cites another seat. Two game-design agents played recorded current worlds through normal controls; root performed live interface and persistence checks. Other seats reviewed shared pixels, factual interaction records and relevant source. Same model family and shared evidence create correlated blind spots. No external humans were contacted; no fresh model generation was performed in this review. No product code changed. A usage-limit interruption was recorded and excluded from substantive review time.

## Detailed summary

### Assessment

- The current build has a coherent creator studio and working recorded games, but this review does not substantiate the earlier all-10/10 quality presentation. Fresh navigation and keyboard probes reveal material editing failures.

- This is a 25-seat simulated specialist review:23 UI/UX lenses and2 game-design lenses. Scores are provisional scoped judgments, not an aggregate product grade, human validation, or winning odds.

### Preserve

- Original artwork remains recognizable and central across illustrated platforming, ordered photo discovery and free photo exploration.

- The restrained neutral/indigo shell, compact primary actions, native controls, optional prompt and review-before-play workflow are useful foundations.

- Normal keyboard/click controls completed current recorded Dino, Clockwork, cat and kitchen games. Preserve forgiving first-play access and the option to continue creating.

- Keep backup/import support, version/progress information, explicit example ownership and configured-versus-tested provider wording.

### Critical Issues

- P1 observed: E11 shows an applied rename lost after opening another world and reopening, without a warning. World updated does not mean saved. Protect this transition and report persistence truthfully.

- P1 observed: E7 shows Delete resetting an existing object's pending rename/move instead of removing it, despite the accessible instruction. Separate reset/removal and provide recoverability.

- P1 interaction: collected-state dimming affects active editor selection. Render selection independently so the creator can see what is being edited.

- P2 observed craft: library count/local-save text clips at the top; mixed-length titles misalign card actions. Correct spacing and alignment without truncating meaningful titles.

### Visual Direction

- Do not replace the palette merely to appear different. Preserve source-driven color and improve semantic contrast, selection visibility, small critical instructions, and spacing where evidence shows a problem.

- Compare more useful artwork area at short desktop sizes with complete keyboard access. Avoid an arbitrary pixel-height target or hiding essential controls.

- Test a source-specific completion treatment with deterministic text contrast, preserving the creator's art. A compact backed panel is one candidate, not an already-proven redesign.

### Ux Content

- Explain the selected gameplay outcome in one line within the existing disclosure. Adapt Update image guidance to the current mode.

- Use action-accurate discovery verbs and clearly indicate the final destination after required details are collected; 5/5 found currently precedes a sixth required cat action.

- Distinguish temporary dialog drafts, unsaved applied changes and durable device saves. Keep disclosures concise and place them where the decision occurs.

- Test compact manual-selection guidance and short object labels while retaining richer clue prose when useful. Do not add a mandatory onboarding wizard.

- Treat reversible role correction and contextual editing routes as deliberate workflow work, with dependency safety; defer speculative library organization.

### Frontend Performance

- Prior six real generation records measured 21.8–55.8 seconds; communicate a qualified wait expectation and slow state without fake progress. No new model generation was run in this review.

- Prior rendering and recorded-response transition measurements support only their bounded workload. They do not certify heavy gameplay, all hardware or inference latency.

- Source shows generic +1 reward translation persists under reduced motion. Verify and correct that branch while preserving static result feedback.

### Agreements

- Protecting edits and making commands truthful outrank optional visual effects and new feature breadth.

- Use persistent completed-state shapes while keeping source pixels recognizable; full-opacity editor selection has a different purpose from subdued playfield completion marks.

- Current generated-world feasibility is evidenced; delight, retention, universal inputs and cross-age comprehension remain unverified.

- A wording-only correction is inadequate for E7 and E11: semantics and state protection must change, with truthful labels reflecting actual behavior.

### Disagreements

- A short forgiving world can be a successful creation payoff. Optional mastery/replay should be tested against source-specific accomplishment feedback; mandatory difficulty is not justified.

- More canvas area is desirable only if target tasks improve without sacrificing control access. Inspector collapse and arbitrary minimum canvas heights are not automatic solutions.

- Some initial recommendations favored library archive/search expansion; QA duplicates do not establish ordinary-user demand, so archive infrastructure is deferred.

- R1 gameplay included an older Dino artifact. GP5 corrects current appearance and actual play evidence; legacy cutout and uncertain focus behavior are excluded from current defect claims.

- Save/discard/stay is a bounded immediate option; recoverable autosave is also viable if it keeps a separate working version, preserves intentional saved snapshots, and handles storage errors.

- Priority changed through debate: gameplay accepted P2 for source-cued collection feedback after motion noted successful completion and no measured route error. The marker remains recommended; reproduced editing loss stays ahead of it.

- Final-clue presentation remains disputed: replace the completed fraction with Final clue remaining, or retain Details found 5/5 alongside that label. Both preserve game rules; the exact wording still needs a small comprehension comparison.

### Sequence

- First: protect unsaved/applied work, separate Delete/reset, preserve recoverability, and make selected objects visible.

- Second: repair visible library craft defects, mode/update copy, discovery finish-state wording, and collection feedback.

- Third: compare desktop space allocation and source-specific completion alternatives, with optional game depth where it serves the creator's intention.

- Verify with a small varied source matrix and actual end-to-end creation/play/edit/save/reopen tasks. Use targeted checks rather than expanding a large unrelated unit suite.

### Validation

- Replay E7 and E11 exactly; record expected versus actual behavior and the resulting visible state. Passing a proposed acceptance criterion is not presumed from implementation.

- Inspect light/dark/busy source artwork, long names and mixed card titles at 1024×600, 1280×720 and 1440×900. Verify meaningful keyboard access and small-object visibility.

- Use 5–20 genuinely varied drawing/photo inputs with all attempts, retries and manual intervention recorded. Separate generation acceptance from playable completion and durable save.

- Observe first-time users for comprehension and optional replay preference before claiming universal ease or psychological attachment. Proposed success criteria are not measured outcomes.

- Implement shared behavior for all worlds and states; use named sample worlds only as reproducible evidence, never as hardcoded production branches.

### Limitations

- Same-family AI agents can share blind spots. No real UI/UX consultants, game designers or hackathon judges reviewed or endorsed this work.

- This review freezes product revision 0a4e3e9. Decisions are recommended actions, not implemented repairs.

- No fresh-generation sample, human age-band study, retention study, full screen-reader run, camera/perspective-photo test, storage-failure injection or broad hardware benchmark was performed. Mobile is deferred per user instruction.

- The prior ten reviewers' narrow repair-scope 10 ratings should not be used as evidence that the overall product meets the requested commercial quality bar. Original records are preserved with a correction, not silently rewritten.

## Decision register

### D01 — adopt (P1)

#### Id

D01

#### Status

adopt

#### Priority

P1

#### Effort

M

#### Owner

Frontend/state owner

#### Proposals

- P-r1-journey-1

- P-r1-navigation-2

- P-r1-trust-2

- P-r2-navigation-1

#### Rationale

Protect applied work before world switching, and distinguish temporary dialog drafts from durable saves. E11 demonstrates loss; a static Save button plus World updated is insufficient.

#### Dissent

Prefer an explicit save/discard/stay guard as the bounded immediate repair, retaining the deliberate manual-save model. Recoverable autosave is a viable separate alternative only with working-versus-saved identity and storage-failure handling. The immediate guard addresses in-app world replacement; it does not itself establish tab-close, crash or reload recovery.

#### Acceptance

- The E11 rename/apply/switch/reopen sequence preserves the change or requires an explicit discard.

- Cancel stays on the changed world. Storage failure does not report success.

- Dialog-close, tab-reload and saved-world guarantees are worded separately.

- Protection uses shared world/revision state, with no test-world names or fixture-specific branches.

- Only navigate after the relevant revision has persisted; failed or obsolete save results cannot replace newer active work.

#### Dependencies

None recorded.

### D02 — adopt (P1)

- New-addition Delete, explicit reset and their accessible instructions match behavior.

- Reset/removal has an understandable recovery path.

- P-r1-research-1

#### Rationale

Use a declared mixed-input attempt matrix and first-time task observation to support broader claims; preserve all failures, retries and repairs.

#### Dissent

Missing human evidence is not itself proof of poor quality. Do not delay immediate defect fixes for a large study or replace product work with a sprawling unit suite.

#### Acceptance

- Exercise5–20 genuinely varied inputs with all attempts and interventions recorded, then trace source-to-play and edit/save/reopen.

- Separate automatic acceptance, manual repair, source fidelity and actual play completion.

- Before claiming all ages or arbitrary-input reliability, obtain relevant user/input evidence; disclose current boundaries.

#### Dependencies

None recorded.

### Gp1

#### Id

GP1

#### Source

screens/gameplay-dino-start.png; screens/gameplay-dino-complete.png; CUA tab 870324876, 2026-11-02

#### Observation

Imported verified-crayon-platform.doodlume.json through My worlds > Import & backup > Import backup > Import 1 world. Opened Dino’s Star Hop. Mission said reach yellow star above tower; intro described purple platforms and red spikes. Clicked Play adventure, then Right 500ms, Space+Right 700ms, Right 650ms, Space+Right 750ms. Visibly reached World complete. The sequence used2.6 seconds of held input; this is not elapsed completion time. The scene retained a large white dinosaur-shaped source cutout while a smaller green dinosaur moved.

#### Limits

Single reviewer and recorded world, no user-study evidence. Screenshots alone do not establish input behavior; notes record actual CUA actions. No fresh generation tested. PROVENANCE CORRECTION: imported pre-recompile legacy Dino backup; source cutout appearance must NOT be treated as final corrected output. ## Full council transcript

## Round 1 — Independent diagnosis

### R1-typography: Typography and readability simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-typography

#### Seat

typography

#### Assessment

Provisional 7.5/10 for desktop typography and readability. The inspected interface has a credible, restrained type system: Manrope headings establish ownership, medium-size action labels scan cleanly, and the create dialog separates title, fields, and optional metadata without excessive emphasis. Both discovery sizes preserve a readable clue row. Commercial refinement falls short where essential example instructions inherit the visual status of a footnote.

#### Strengths

- E2 screens 01–05: consistent heading weight, dark text, and generous separation make the principal actions and world identity immediately legible.

- E2 screens 02 and 03: the discovery clue remains readable on its own solid surface at both supplied desktop sizes; screen 05 keeps optional metadata subordinate without obscuring field labels.

#### Concerns

- Judgment: screen 04 gives the specific win-condition instructions less typographic prominence than the generic “Complete the required actions” row. The compact two-line caption is legible when inspected, but asks a new player to read consequential details at a smaller size than ordinary controls.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-typography-1

##### Action

Increase the adventure launch instruction to the established 14px body size with approximately 21px leading, keeping its solid backing and bounded line length.

##### Rationale

The specific task is prerequisite reading and deserves at least the readability afforded to ordinary interface labels.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E2

##### Validation

Inspect the example at 1280×720 and 1024×600: all instructions remain visible without truncation, collisions, or covering the play button; ask first-time players to restate the required and optional tasks before starting.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

#### Position

Preserve the existing family, compact title tracking, and restrained hierarchy. This is a targeted increase in instructional emphasis, not evidence for a wholesale type redesign.

#### Uncertainty

No live zoom, text enlargement, font-loading, long-title, or assistive-technology testing was performed. Pixel inspection supports appearance judgments; actual reading speed and task comprehension remain unverified.

#### Rating

##### Score

7.5

##### Scope

Typography/readability of supplied desktop studio, discovery, example, and creation screens

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Consistent and visibly readable hierarchy across five captures, with one meaningful imbalance in the prominence of essential game instructions; uninspected editing states constrain confidence.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-typography-1

##### Kind

judgment

##### Severity

P2

##### Location

screens/04-example.png, launch caption below Play world; src/studio.css:448–457; src/App.tsx:1397

##### Observation

Fact: the launch caption uses 12px text and 18px leading, confirmed in CSS, for instructions distinguishing required seeds, optional climbing, and a switch. Judgment: this is too subordinate relative to the larger generic objective row.

##### Impact

New players can overlook or under-read the specific task; comprehension loss is a hypothesis requiring user testing, not an observed behavioral failure.

##### Correction

Use 14px/21px instructional body text and preserve contrast and bounded measure.

##### Acceptance

At the two target desktop sizes the whole instruction is readable and visible before play; new players can identify the required seeds and the optional goal without searching another control.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E2

---

### R1-layout: Composition, alignment and spacing simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-layout

#### Seat

layout

#### Assessment

Provisional 7/10 for desktop composition, alignment, spacing and density. The inspected screens have a coherent spatial system: restrained borders, consistent modal padding, clear action footers and stable two-column workspaces. Commercial refinement is limited by how aggressively the short desktop composition sacrifices the artwork to surrounding controls.

#### Strengths

- 07/08-editor: the canvas and 260px inspector align cleanly; selected-object details remain contained and the footer separates editing from committing.

- 09-create-empty: image and idea columns share a clear top alignment; the large source drop zone correctly receives the strongest spatial emphasis.

- 12-manual-arrangement: equal source/layout panels and aligned headings make the relationship between artwork selection and arrangement visible. This observation is appearance only.

#### Concerns

- 03-discovery-short: the open keyboard controls occupy a roughly 110px footer while the actual fitted artwork is only about 324×216px. Controls remain visible, but artwork inspection is visually subordinate at a supported desktop size.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- E3

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-layout-1

##### Action

Rework short-desktop vertical allocation so expanded keyboard play preserves a larger artwork surface, using a more compact control arrangement and reduced heading/section spacing while retaining visible controls.

##### Rationale

The game asks people to inspect painterly detail; the fitted source loses too much area at 1024×600 when secondary controls expand.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E2

##### Validation

Capture identical discovery states at 1024×600 and 1440×900 with keyboard controls open and closed. At 1024×600 target at least 280px artwork height without clipping controls, horizontal scrolling, or requiring Focus on play; verify keyboard controls remain operable.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

M

---

#### Position

Preserve the two-column editors and consistent modal/action framing. Prioritize short-desktop allocation over a general visual redesign.

#### Uncertainty

Static pixels and relevant CSS were inspected, including 02/03, 07/08, 09 and 12. No live interaction, user task timing, expanded editor details or populated manual arrangement was assessed. The proposed 280px target is a design acceptance hypothesis, not a measured usability threshold.

#### Rating

##### Score

7

##### Scope

Desktop composition, alignment, spacing and density across supplied play/create/editor/manual screens

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Coherent alignment and modal hierarchy are positively evidenced; short desktop artwork allocation remains materially weak for a detail-inspection game.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-layout-1

##### Kind

expert judgment

##### Severity

P2

##### Location

screens/03-discovery-short.png, discovery artwork and open Keyboard play footer; src/studio.css:2623–2639

##### Observation

At 1024×600 the artwork occupies approximately x350–674,y199–415, while the control footer occupies y415–525. The main layout is viewport-height-constrained and the stage receives residual flex space.

##### Impact

Small visual details are harder to inspect and the artwork loses the visual prominence expected from an image-led playable product. Actual task error rates are unmeasured.

##### Correction

Reduce short-desktop chrome height and compact the expanded controls without hiding the keyboard alternative; preserve a meaningful minimum artwork area.

##### Acceptance

At 1024×600 with keyboard play open, identical 3:2 art fits at least 280px high and all controls remain visible and operable without overlap; compare 1440×900 for regression.

Provisional 8.0/10 for desktop color, contrast and visual hierarchy across supplied screens 01–16. The restrained white and warm-gray chrome gives uploaded artwork ownership of the experience. Indigo consistently identifies creation, continuation and active tool states; the dark editor surround provides useful separation without tinting the source. This is coherent commercial craft. The weakness is concentrated in artwork overlays, where interface colors and opacity must survive unpredictable source imagery and completion state.

#### Strengths

- Primary actions are clearly differentiated in retained creation, alignment and completion (05, 14–15, 06). Disabled actions have visibly different treatment in empty creation and drawing (09, 11).

- Selected drawing color and alignment corner have additional outlines, while labels accompany status and actions. Completion keeps legible light text on a strongly darkened background.

#### Concerns

##### Rationale

The editable target should remain immediately identifiable after completing a discovery world, including over multicolored artwork.

##### Evidence

- E1

P2

#### Rating

##### Score

8.0

##### Scope

Desktop color, contrast and hierarchy; screenshots 01–16 and relevant rendering source

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Strong consistent chrome and primary-action hierarchy; materially weaker selected-region visibility after discovery completion prevents exceptional refinement.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-color-1

##### Kind

observed defect with source-supported mechanism

##### Severity

P2

##### Location

screens/08-editor-selected.png; src/components/WorldEditor.tsx object(), collected-entity opacity and selected outline

##### Observation

The selected region has very weak visible selection emphasis after completion. object() assigns opacity 0.14 to collected coins at the parent group, so the subsequently rendered selection rectangle inherits the same dimming. Unselected discovery frames use green #55763d against green watercolor.

##### Impact

A creator can read the inspector but struggle to identify the corresponding editable area, reducing confidence in moving or resizing the intended region.

##### Correction

Keep historical/collected artwork dimming separate from editor selection overlays; use full-opacity selection with a light/dark keyline and visible selected handle or marker.

##### Acceptance

After completing a discovery world, selecting each region makes its boundary unambiguous at fit and 100% zoom; the outline remains clearly perceptible over light, dark and similarly colored imagery without covering the artwork.

##### Evidence

- E1: WorldEditor.tsx lines 776–789, 797–807, 847–860

- E3: screens/08-editor-selected.png

### R1-brand: Distinctiveness and visual identity simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-brand

#### Seat

brand

#### Assessment

Provisional 7/10 for desktop visual identity and commercial craft across studio, create, drawing, editor, manual arrangement, library and completion. Doodlume has a credible restrained foundation: a compact rounded wordmark, strong titles, cobalt actions and abundant space let original art carry the experience. The clockwork scene and watercolor editor visibly belong to different creators while retaining one coherent application. This is a valuable identity strategy. Its limitation is at the product-owned moments: creation uses a conventional upload form and completion relies on a generic sparkle badge and “World complete.” These are competent interfaces, but the observed completion does little to make the source-to-play transformation memorable. That is a design judgment, not evidence of failed brand recognition.

#### Strengths

- Original art dominates the studio and editor; neutral surfaces accommodate very different palettes without competing with them.

- The compact wordmark and consistent heavy titles provide a recognisable shell without adding decorative clutter.

- Manual arrangement keeps source image and game layout adjacent, visibly expressing the product’s central creative relationship.

#### Concerns

- The completed watercolor world is substantially darkened beneath a generic badge and message; the most celebratory state suppresses the asset that distinguishes this product.

- The library’s clipped count and uneven Open/Backup rows weaken the otherwise orderly gallery presentation.

#### Evidence

- E2

- E3

- E4

- E5

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-brand-1

##### Action

Test a source-led completion treatment within the existing layout: retain a clearly visible source detail and make the short success label specific to the completed world or activity; preserve the current calm shell and action hierarchy.

##### Rationale

The user’s own work is the most credible distinctive brand asset. Completion can reinforce that relationship without introducing mascots, ornamental backgrounds or a wholesale redesign.

##### Evidence

- E3

##### Validation

Compare the existing and revised watercolor completion at 1280×720; the source should remain readily identifiable, success and primary action immediately legible, and no extra interaction required. Confirm the treatment accommodates another world with very different artwork.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

#### Position

Preserve the neutral authoring direction and art-first composition. Improve a small number of product-owned moments before considering broader visual restyling.

#### Uncertainty

Screenshots establish appearance only. Recognition, preference, perceived commercial credibility and emotional response have not been tested with users; motion and sound were not assessed.

#### Rating

##### Score

7

##### Scope

Desktop visual identity and commercial craft across supplied studio/create/editor/manual/library/completion pixels

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Coherent restrained foundation with expressive source artwork, but limited ownable treatment at completion and visible gallery finishing defects.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-brand-1

##### Kind

expert-judgment

##### Severity

P2

##### Location

screens/06-completion.png, central completed-world overlay

##### Observation

A pale sparkle badge and generic “World complete” appear over heavily dimmed watercolor art. The same frame’s title is specific to watercolor, but the completion treatment itself is visually generic.

##### Impact

The payoff gives less visual credit to the user’s artwork and the image-to-game transformation than the studio does; reduced memorability is a hypothesis requiring comparison.

##### Correction

Test a restrained source-led completion treatment using the existing artwork and activity-specific short success label.

##### Acceptance

At 1280×720, source art remains recognisable while success and actions retain clear contrast; compare at least two visually different worlds and seek preference/recall feedback before standardising.

### R1-interaction: Interaction consistency and affordances simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-interaction

#### Seat

interaction

#### Assessment

Provisional 6.8/10 for desktop interaction consistency, affordances and feedback across edit, play completion, library, More and image update. The ordinary actions are understandable, but the editor weakens its own selection feedback after success and gives Delete two different meanings. These are material creative-control problems at a commercial standard, even though the surrounding dialogs are calm and orderly.

#### Strengths

- Completion offers distinct creation, continued exploration and replay actions; the image-update form separates source choice from review and application.

- Editor drafts explicitly survive closing; Apply changes is disabled without changes. Library opening and backup have separate labeled targets, and the common modal implements Escape and focus restoration.

#### Concerns

- Collected regions inherit gameplay fading inside the editor, including their selection outline. In the selected completed-world capture, the uncollected region is far more legible than the object actually selected.

- Delete is announced as removing the selected object, but existing objects instead lose every draft modification; only new objects are removed. Per-object reset, last-addition removal and world-level undo form different recovery scopes without stepwise draft undo.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E5

- E6

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-interaction-1

##### Action

Render editor selection in a separate full-opacity layer, independent of collected/open gameplay state.

##### Rationale

The selected object must remain the clearest interaction target after a completed game.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E3

##### Validation

Select found and unfound discovery regions at fit and focused zoom; both show an equally clear outline and handles without changing earned progress.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

S

---

##### Id

P-r1-interaction-2

Make Delete remove only removable additions; give existing-object reset an explicit command and accurate keyboard guidance, with draft undo for individual edits.

##### Rationale

A familiar destructive key must not silently revert an unrelated set of name, position and size edits.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E3

##### Validation

Rename and move an existing region, press Delete, and verify no silent reset; Undo reverses one latest edit while preserving earlier edits. New additions retain clearly explained removal.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

M

---

#### Position

Initial blind review. Preserve the concise dialog structure and explicit apply boundary; improve the editor's feedback and recovery semantics before cosmetic expansion.

#### Uncertainty

I inspected supplied PNGs/AX text and current source, not a live session. Update submission, library opening, save latency and draft recovery were not exercised. Selection weakness is directly visible and source-supported; recovery consequences are source-derived and require live verification.

#### Rating

##### Score

6.8

##### Scope

Desktop interaction consistency, affordances and feedback in supplied edit/play/library/More/update evidence

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Clear primary actions and review boundaries are undermined by weak selected-object feedback and inconsistent editor reset/removal semantics.

screens/08-editor-selected.png; src/components/WorldEditor.tsx:776-859

##### Observation

Open Edit world after completing Watercolor Splash Discovery and choose the peach/coral looping arc. The screenshot shows the uncollected lower-right region clearly while the chosen region's outline is barely legible. object() applies opacity 0.14 to the entire collected coin group, including its selected outline.

##### Id

F-r1-interaction-2

##### Kind

observed defect (source-derived, live verification pending)

##### Severity

medium

##### Location

src/components/WorldEditor.tsx:715-724,1098,1194-1200

##### Observation

Select an existing object, change name and position, focus canvas and press Delete. canvasKey calls removeSelected, which deletes its entire modified entry rather than removing the object. The canvas accessible instructions nevertheless say 'Delete removes it.' For a newly added object the same key actually removes it.

##### Impact

Keyboard users receive false guidance and can unexpectedly lose multiple accepted draft adjustments. Recovery is reset-whole-object or discard-whole-draft rather than one-step undo.

##### Correction

Separate reset from removal and describe each scope accurately; provide per-edit undo.

##### Acceptance

Delete never silently resets existing-object edits; reset is explicitly named; Undo restores the immediately previous draft state without erasing earlier changes.

##### Evidence

- E1

### R1-navigation: Information architecture and navigation simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-navigation

#### Seat

navigation

#### Assessment

Provisional 6.5/10 for desktop information architecture and discoverability. The studio has a coherent world-first hierarchy: My worlds, New world, Original, Edit world and Save are readable, stable destinations. Creation progressively discloses gameplay options, and completion offers useful next actions. However, the creative loop is split across Edit world and an unlabeled ellipsis containing Update image and Rules. The editor itself offers no route to those sibling tasks. This is a meaningful information-scent weakness for a product whose promise depends on evolving the same drawing into the same game. Draft recovery also has inconsistent visibility: Resume draft represents a new creation, while retained existing-world editor and artwork drafts have no analogous outer indication. Saving is easy to find, but the persistent interface does not explain whether the displayed revision has been saved. These are commercial refinement gaps, not evidence that routes are broken. I would improve contextual labels and state cues before adding navigation chrome.

#### Strengths

- Global library access and visible world-scoped Edit/Save commands establish a compact hierarchy (screens 01–04).

- Update image explicitly promises same-world/progress continuity, shows current versus revised artwork, and names its review step (21).

### R1-onboarding: First-use comprehension simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-onboarding

#### Seat

onboarding

#### Assessment

Provisional 6.8/10 for first-use comprehension across the example landing state, creation dialog, drawing workspace, and source-inspected handoff to preview. The product gives a clear immediate choice: play a labeled example or bring your own image. However, the novice must infer what constitutes a useful drawing and what the gameplay names actually produce. These are meaningful expectation-setting gaps in the core promise, even though the controls themselves are clean.

#### Strengths

- The first-view headline, Draw here and Upload image actions make personal creation prominent; Example · Adventure and Make a copy distinguish the supplied world from user ownership (04-example).

- The empty create form makes the image requirement visible, labels the idea optional, and keeps advanced gameplay choices collapsed. The drawing footer explains draft retention and names the next action Use this drawing.

- Source inspection shows a preview-and-review stage with rules, Edit objects and Play this world, plus retained inputs after creation errors; this is a useful progression to preserve, although the complete generated flow was not exercised.

#### Concerns

- The first drawing surface offers no example of a suitable scene or explanation of how its content becomes gameplay. Blank-canvas uncertainty is a hypothesis requiring novice observation, not a measured failure.

- Adventure, Explore and Discover have materially different behavior in creationIntent(), but their expanded controls expose names only. Explore and Discover are especially easy to treat as synonyms.

- E1

- E2

- E3

- E4

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-onboarding-1

##### Action

Add a concise contextual explanation for the selected gameplay choice, including what Auto decides; retain the collapsed default.

##### Rationale

The implementation distinguishes jumping, free movement and finding objects in the original image, but the selector does not communicate that distinction.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E4

##### Validation

Inspect all four selected states; in a planned novice comprehension check, ask participants to predict whether each mode uses a moving character or object-finding and whether jumping is involved, before generation. Record misunderstandings rather than treating this review as validation.

P1

##### Effort

S

---

P-r1-onboarding-2

Provide one optional drawing-start hint or tiny source-to-game example at the blank canvas, and a short indication that the drawing will next be reviewed as an image before creating its game.

The empty canvas currently explains tools and preservation, but does not help a novice form a viable first input or anticipate the next step.

##### Evidence

- E2

- E4

- E1

##### Validation

A planned first-use session should establish whether participants can choose their own scene and describe the drawing → image/idea → preview → play progression without facilitator instruction; test both drawing and photo paths.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

Initial independent assessment. Preserve the compact ownership cues, optional idea field, progressive disclosure and reversible preview. Improve comprehension at the moment a choice is made rather than adding a mandatory tour.

### R1-editing: Creative editing and precision simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-editing

#### Seat

editing

#### Assessment

Provisional 6/10 for desktop creative editing and precision. The editor gives real control over names, position, dimensions and added-object roles; source imagery remains central. Manual arrangement clearly separates source selection from game placement, and Update image presents a legible before/revised comparison. However, commercial creative control requires reliable selection feedback and reversible experiments. In the supplied completed discovery scene, selected collected regions are visually almost absent while an uncollected region remains prominent. Delete silently resets existing-object edits despite promising removal. More fundamentally, existing roles are immutable and existing objects cannot be removed through this editor. That protects references, but leaves creators unable to correct a mistaken interpretation after creation. The visible controls feel like a guarded revision form more than a complete creative workspace.

#### Strengths

- Numeric X/Y/width/height and one-unit Shift+arrow movement support precise correction; fit, focus and pan support larger scenes.

- Object selector plus wrapping name field preserves access to long labels; draft retention and explicit Apply separate exploration from commitment.

- Manual setup exposes eight roles, source crop and layout separately; Update image offers drawing, upload and camera with review before application.

#### Concerns

- Existing-object Delete contradicts its exact accessible instruction and discards all draft changes for that object.

- Selection chrome inherits collected/open state opacity, weakening spatial confirmation precisely when editing a completed world.

- Existing roles/removal and granular draft undo are missing, restricting correction and safe experimentation.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E4

- E6

- E7

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-editing-1

##### Action

Make existing-object Delete behavior explicit and separate full-opacity selection chrome from progress fading.

##### Rationale

The user must see the exact target and predict the command before editing.

##### Evidence

- E3

- E7

- E1

##### Validation

Select a collected and uncollected region on busy artwork; both show equally clear selection outlines. Accessible Delete instructions match the actual operation for existing and new objects.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

S

---

##### Id

P-r1-editing-2

##### Action

Add reversible role/removal correction for established objects, with explicit handling of dependent rules, and action-level draft undo/redo.

##### Rationale

Creators need to repair generated interpretations and compare placements without losing unrelated work.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E4

- E6

- E7

##### Validation

Correct an existing collectible to scenery and remove an unwanted region; review affected requirements, apply, and undo safely. Within a draft, undo one move while preserving an earlier rename and redo it.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

L

---

#### Position

Initial blind assessment. Preserve source-centered authoring and progressive disclosure of numeric controls; address command trust and reversible creative control before adding decorative UI.

#### Uncertainty

I inspected supplied screenshots/text and frozen source, did not operate the browser, and did not submit an update. Applied-update preservation, manual authored playability, pointer ergonomics and user comprehension remain unverified. Findings distinguish supplied live reproduction from source-observed limitations.

observed defect

WorldEditor.tsx:715-724,1098; screens/22-editor-before-delete and 23-editor-after-delete

### R1-recovery: Errors, cancellation and recovery simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-recovery

#### Seat

recovery

#### Assessment

Provisional 7/10 for desktop error, cancellation and recovery design. Recovery has a credible foundation: creation preserves inputs, cancellation rejects stale results, and image updates separate review from application. The inspected evidence nevertheless contains one actual loss of temporary editing work, while update-request errors offer weaker guidance than creation errors. This is a solid implementation direction, with insufficient exercised failure coverage for an exceptional commercial-quality score.

#### Strengths

- Screens 05 and 09 distinguish a populated creation draft from an empty form; screen 21 states that closing keeps the draft and that review precedes application.

- App.tsx:718-790 uses AbortController and request identity checks, preserves image/idea on failure, and offers Try again or Stop creating. shared/revisions.ts validates revision identity, retains compatible progress, and rejects unsafe character overlap before returning an applied world. These are source-confirmed safeguards, not fresh failure tests.

#### Concerns

- E7 demonstrates that Delete silently resets an existing object’s pending name and placement changes; the advertised removal does not occur.

- PaperUpdate.tsx:104-107 displays raw Error.message for failed requests, unlike the actionable network-error translation in App.tsx:768-773.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- E3

- E6

- E7

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-recovery-1

##### Action

Make Delete semantics truthful and preserve a recoverable previous draft state when resetting an existing object.

##### Rationale

An accidental key press currently erases pending edits while leaving the selected object present. A commercial editor must make this reversal legible and recoverable.

##### Evidence

- E7

- E1

##### Validation

Rename and move an existing region, press Delete, then verify that the announced action matches the result and that the previous draft can be restored without altering the live world. Check new additions separately.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

##### Id

P-r1-recovery-2

##### Action

Give image-update network failures the same retained-draft reassurance and concrete retry/manual-placement guidance as creation.

##### Rationale

An update failure should tell the author what remains safe and how to proceed; forwarding a browser error does neither reliably.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E6

##### Validation

Use controlled failed requests, cancellation followed by a late response, and successful retry; verify that image, intent, live world and progress remain unchanged until Apply. Record actual captures before claiming recovery coverage.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

#### Position

Preserve preview-before-apply, cancellation controls, local draft retention and revision guards. Repair the observed reset mismatch and normalize update-failure guidance before claiming refined recovery.

#### Uncertainty

No browser interaction or paid requests performed by this reviewer. Screens 05/09/21 are form states, not failed-request or cancellation evidence. E7 is the supplied live reproduction. Offline retries, storage failure, crash/reload recovery, cancellation races and applied image-update progress retention were not exercised. Editor and paper drafts use in-memory maps; closing a dialog is not evidence of persistence across reload.

7

Desktop errors, cancellation, draft preservation and revision recovery at frozen 0a4e3e9

##### Confidence

medium

### R1-accessibility: Keyboard and semantic accessibility simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-accessibility

#### Seat

accessibility

#### Assessment

Provisional 6.8/10 for desktop accessibility in the inspected drawing, manual arrangement, editor, rules and library surfaces. Native controls and useful alternatives show deliberate accessibility work, but the editor gives a materially false keyboard instruction. This is a source, pixel and supplied accessibility-tree review, not screen-reader certification.

#### Strengths

- Rules expose named checkboxes and clue textboxes; the selected state is visible through checkmarks as well as color. Library Open buttons include world name, version and saved time in their accessible names.

- Manual arrangement has Add selection plus numeric game/crop controls; editor supplies an object selector, keyboard movement, zoom and numeric properties. Drawing tools expose named ink choices and pressed states.

- Modal.tsx uses native showModal with named headings and focus-return handling. WorldScene.ts explicitly respects reduced motion for atmosphere, camera and effects; these remain source-supported strengths rather than tested outcomes.

#### Concerns

- The canvas promises Delete removes the object, while existing objects instead lose their draft changes. This is especially misleading for someone relying on spoken canvas instructions.

- Manual arrangement explains only dragging even though an alternative exists. A keyboard user must infer that Add selection creates an editable default region.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E4

- E5

- E7

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-accessibility-1

##### Action

Make the editor keyboard instruction conditional on existing versus newly added selection, and announce the resulting reset or removal.

##### Rationale

Keyboard instructions must accurately describe the destructive effect on draft changes. Preserve the currently accurate Reset selected object changes button label.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E7

##### Validation

Select an existing object, change its name and position, focus canvas and press Delete; verify reset instruction and announced result. Repeat with a new addition and verify removal instruction and result.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

S

---

##### Id

P-r1-accessibility-2

##### Action

Add concise manual arrangement guidance: use Add selection, then adjust Selection and Game fields; preserve native controls and reduced-motion branches.

##### Rationale

The existing alternative is valuable but is hidden behind drag-only guidance.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E4

##### Validation

Complete first character and destination placement with keyboard alone, including crop adjustment, and verify focus location after Add selection.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

#### Position

Maintain the existing native-control approach and improve accurate discoverability before adding custom interaction machinery.

#### Uncertainty

Inspected screenshots and supplied AX text for drawing, manual arrangement, selected editor, rules and library. No live keyboard traversal, assistive-technology output, text zoom, contrast sampling or motion-preference run was performed. Screenshot text is visually legible at captured size, but contrast compliance is unmeasured. Drawing canvas is pointer-based with upload mentioned as an alternative; equivalent creative outcomes are unverified.

#### Rating

##### Score

6.8

##### Scope

Desktop keyboard and semantic affordances across drawing/manual/editor/rules/library, with source-only motion review

##### Confidence

moderate

##### Reason

Strong native labels and alternative manipulation controls, offset by confirmed misleading keyboard action instructions and weak manual alternative guidance; behavior and contrast coverage remain incomplete.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-accessibility-1

##### Kind

observed defect

##### Severity

P1

##### Location

src/components/WorldEditor.tsx:715-730,1098; screens/08-editor-selected.txt; E7

##### Observation

Canvas label says Delete removes it. removeSelected deletes only the modified record for existingSelected. Root E7 reproduced restoration of original name and disabling Apply after Delete.

##### Impact

Users following accessible instructions can unknowingly discard all pending edits to an existing object while expecting deletion.

Describe Reset for existing objects and Remove for additions, and expose a concise live result.

##### Acceptance

Existing and new selections each expose the accurate action before activation and an unambiguous outcome after keyboard activation.

### R1-inclusive: Inclusive age and language usability simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-inclusive

#### Seat

inclusive

#### Assessment

Provisional 6/10 for inclusive desktop comprehension across reading confidence and language familiarity. The image-first form is approachable, but generated discovery language and manual setup instructions still require substantial interpretation. This is a source-and-screen review, not evidence of any age group's actual performance.

#### Strengths

- Create a world makes Your idea optional and offers image upload, drawing and phone capture; a user need not compose a written prompt to begin (E3/E4).

- Discovery offers zoom, a visible hint action and a native object selector; WorldCanvas.tsx:411-459 also focuses the selected region, providing useful alternatives to precise pointing (E1/E2).

- Manual Character guidance explains its role concretely as the one you move and jump with; object names remain editable (E3/E4).

#### Concerns

- Observed labels use long, specialized color descriptions; expert judgment is that these impose unnecessary decoding and memory burdens on people less familiar with English or art vocabulary, regardless of age.

- Manual setup says Source artwork required while the uploaded artwork is already visible. The intended distinction between uploading an image and marking a character within it is insufficiently explicit.

- Inspected chrome and settings are English; index.html declares lang=en. Multilingual generation, translated interface coverage and right-to-left behavior remain unverified, so cross-language inclusiveness cannot be certified.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- E3

- E4

- E5

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-inclusive-1

##### Action

Provide short, plain object names alongside optional richer clues, and rename manual empty-state guidance to describe the next selection action. Preserve optional prompt entry and existing keyboard discovery.

##### Rationale

Reduce unnecessary language inference without reducing creative descriptions or removing alternative input paths.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- E3

- E4

##### Validation

On the watercolor fixture, verify every object has an unambiguous short name with full detail still available. In manual setup with an uploaded image and zero objects, show Draw a box around your character in the image; validate comprehension with participants of varied reading confidence and English familiarity without assuming ability from age.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

M

---

Initial blind review. Preserve the image-first and keyboard alternatives; prioritize plain naming and accurate next-step language before claiming broad audience readiness.

#### Uncertainty

No live play, human usability sessions, age-specific outcomes, translation trials, assistive-technology sessions or full keyboard-only authoring were assessed. Numeric manual controls exist in source; this review does not claim manual creation is pointer-only. Existing fixture wording is not proof that all newly generated worlds use the same language complexity.

#### Rating

##### Score

6

##### Scope

Desktop inclusive comprehension and input choices in the supplied create, discovery, editor and manual arrangement experience

##### Confidence

medium

##### Impact

### R1-content: Microcopy and content design simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-content

#### Seat

content

#### Assessment

6.8/10 provisional for desktop microcopy and information relevance. The product usually speaks in useful verbs: choose an image, draw here, apply changes, and replay from start. “Your image. Your game.” quickly explains the creative promise. World, game, image, and draft are mostly coherent: world names the saved creation, image names the input, and draft names unapplied work. I would preserve this vocabulary rather than impose a global rename. The weakness is guidance at consequential decisions. Explore and Discover are near-synonyms without an explanation of their different interaction models. The watercolor discovery update asks about a bridge and lever, borrowing advice from a different game type. Most seriously, the editor promises that Delete removes an object although the recorded existing-object action resets its edits. These are specific content mismatches, not a general excess-of-copy problem. Repeated draft reassurance appears at different risk points and is useful. Provider/model identifiers in Settings serve diagnosis, although their usefulness to an ordinary creator is untested; burying all technical details is not justified by these captures. Progress totals omit the final destination, but the clue continues to guide the user, so I treat the confusion risk as secondary evidence rather than claim users cannot finish.

#### Strengths

- Clear action labels and the image-to-game proposition keep most flows concrete (screens 04, 09, 11, 21).

- Draft-retention and local-save messages explain consequences at the relevant moment (screens 11, 17, 21, 22).

- Rules explicitly distinguish required actions, optional actions, and the final destination; descriptive artwork clues refer to visible color and shape (screen 19).

#### Concerns

- C-content-1: Gameplay choices lack their decision-making distinction.

- C-content-2: Update guidance assumes an adventure even in Discovery.

- C-content-3: The editor accessibility instruction promises removal where Delete resets existing-object edits.

A creator should be able to predict whether they will move a character or follow clues before spending a generation request; subsequent advice should match that choice.

### R1-performance: Perceived speed and waiting experience simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-performance

#### Seat

performance

#### Assessment

Provisional 7/10 for perceived speed, waiting and error feedback in the inspected desktop creation/display flows. Local rendering evidence is encouraging, but waiting for genuine creation is a materially different experience: six recorded pipelines required 21.8–55.8 seconds. The interface acknowledges work and preserves agency, yet supplies little context for that substantial pause.

#### Strengths

- Creation exposes elapsed seconds, Stop creating, retained image/idea and Try again after errors. Abort handling ignores superseded results; the server aborts on disconnect and imposes a 120-second ceiling (App.tsx:705–789; server/index.ts:119–169).

- Artwork startup escalates after three seconds, fails with recovery guidance after fifteen seconds, and retains source/world/progress. Recorded local first-world readiness was 882.5ms and recorded-response review-to-play was 117.1ms (WorldCanvas.tsx:225–238; E9).

#### Concerns

- Creating your world plus a rising elapsed count does not tell a first-time creator whether a 40–55-second wait is ordinary or stalled. No upfront duration expectation or delayed-state explanation appears in this creation UI (App.tsx:1091–1094,1768–1795). This is a commercial refinement gap, not evidence that the pipeline failed.

- The excellent 8.3ms median/9.3ms p95 frame sample covers mostly standing rendering. It cannot establish sustained complex-play smoothness, input responsiveness or cold-network speed; artificial request delay and review dwell must not be reported as real processing latency.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E9

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-performance-1

##### Action

Add concise, evidence-qualified creation-wait expectations and a distinct slow-request message while preserving elapsed time, Stop creating and draft-safe retry. Use actual backend milestones only if available; avoid simulated progress percentages.

##### Rationale

A 21.8–55.8-second observed creation interval needs expectation management so healthy processing is distinguishable from an unexplained stall.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E9

##### Validation

Exercise delayed success at 25 and 55 seconds, server timeout, lost network, cancellation and retry with controlled fixtures. Verify accurate status, retained image/idea, no stale result application and comprehensible recovery; separately validate any duration claim on representative real runs.

##### Priority

P2

##### Effort

S

---

#### Position

Preserve the responsive local display and explicit recovery controls. Improve creation-wait communication before treating fast rendering as evidence of an exceptionally refined end-to-end experience.

#### Uncertainty

Source and supplied neutral timings only; no new live interaction, paid requests or user waiting study. Render timings are local, mostly idle and from an earlier current-runtime session. Update-image comparison has cancellation and retained drafts but no supplied duration benchmark.

#### Rating

##### Score

7

##### Scope

Desktop perceived speed, creation waiting and display recovery; excludes universal renderer performance and overall product quality

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Useful cancellation, retention and bounded display recovery support a solid experience; long genuine creation waits remain under-explained, and workload coverage is limited.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-performance-1

##### Kind

expert judgment

##### Severity

P2

##### Location

src/App.tsx:1091–1094 and 1768–1795; E9 real-pipeline-timings.json

##### Observation

During creation, source renders a static Creating your world label, spinner and elapsed seconds. The six genuine pipeline records span 21.8–55.8 seconds; the creation state contains no duration expectation or slow-state transition.

##### Impact

Users may interpret an ordinary long request as stalled and abandon or cancel useful work. This consequence is a hypothesis requiring user validation, not an observed abandonment rate.

##### Correction

Give a qualified expectation before submission and contextual reassurance when the request becomes slow, preserving safe cancellation and retry.

##### Acceptance

At controlled 25s and 55s delays, the UI truthfully explains continued waiting and keeps Stop creating reachable. Timeout/network error preserves source and idea, exposes retry, and never applies a cancelled response. Any promised duration is supported by representative measurements.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E9

The library is visually approachable and gives artwork priority, but it behaves like a small recent-files gallery rather than a durable creative collection. #### Strengths

- Large source-image thumbnails support recognition and preserve the creator’s original art. Explicit Open world and Back up world labels make the two card actions distinguishable.

- The count and local-device storage statement are visibly clipped at the top of the scroll body. This damages both polish and the visibility of an important persistence limitation.

- There is no search, filter, rename or removal control in WorldLibrary. Source sorts by saved time and permits imported copies without a retention cap. Repeated artwork therefore accumulates with little way to curate or retrieve it.

- Two-line titles visibly move Open and Backup down relative to neighboring cards, weakening the scan rhythm.

### R1-journey: End-to-end service and task continuity simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-journey

#### Seat

journey

#### Assessment

Provisional 6.5/10 for desktop create–review–play–edit–save–reopen continuity. The experience has a coherent creative loop: source inspection and object repair precede play; completion offers creation, exploration and replay; library cards show completion, version and saved time. The material weakness is the boundary between applying work and preserving work. A creator can apply an edit, see “World updated,” then open a library item and replace that active revision without an unsaved-state warning. This is supported by source inspection, not a newly performed loss test. The interface gives Save a stable label and no visible saved/changed status, making persistence responsibility easy to miss.

#### Strengths

- Review pairs original image with playable interpretation and exposes object/rule repair before acceptance (App.tsx:917–950,1905–2008).

- Completion presents three distinct continuations; saved records preserve play state and undo checkpoints; reopening uses retained play state rather than blindly resetting progress.

- Create screenshot retains source and idea, while editor source explicitly retains a closed draft within the session.

#### Concerns

- Applied changes and newly accepted worlds exist outside the saved library until an explicit Save; switching worlds has no dirty-state guard.

- The inspected completion screen celebrates achievement but provides no contextual indication whether the achievement or current edits are saved.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- E3

- E5

- E10

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-journey-1

##### Action

Expose durable saved/unsaved status and preserve or explicitly resolve unsaved work before replacing the active world. Make the post-apply notice say changes need saving if persistence remains manual.

##### Rationale

The creative loop should not silently drop its latest outcome at a normal navigation boundary.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E5

- E10

##### Validation

On a saved world, change an object, apply, open another world, and return; either the revision survives or the transition explicitly resolves saving/discarding. Repeat with a newly accepted world and completed progress, including storage failure. Verify status changes only after successful persistence.

##### Priority

P1

##### Effort

M

---

#### Position

Preserve the source-review gate and completion continuations. Prioritize persistence communication and navigation protection before adding more journey branches.

#### Uncertainty

I inspected supplied pixels and relevant source only. I did not interact with the browser, generate a world, exercise saving, reload, or play. Fresh end-to-end persistence, storage failure recovery, and first-time comprehension remain unverified. Screenshot appearance does not prove behavior.

#### Rating

##### Score

6.5

##### Scope

Desktop end-to-end task continuity and persistence handoffs

##### Confidence

medium

##### Reason

Clear stage transitions and recovery-oriented source exist, but manual persistence has an unprotected replacement boundary; live end-to-end creation and reopen were not tested by this seat.

#### Findings

##### Id

F-r1-journey-1

##### Kind

source-supported defect

##### Severity

P1

##### Location

src/App.tsx:443–475 loadWorld; 512–554 keepRevision/applyEdit; 618–650 save; 917–950 review acceptance; 2158–2166 library onOpen; 1280–1289 Save control

##### Observation

keepRevision updates React state and closes the editor; applyEdit announces “World updated.” Only save persists the world/state/history through updateLibrary. Library onOpen calls loadWorld directly, overwriting active world/history/state. Save text does not distinguish dirty versus persisted state; no beforeunload or unsaved guard was found in App.tsx.

##### Impact

An accepted new world or applied revision can be lost through ordinary world switching or reload before Save, undermining trust in the create/play/edit loop. User expectation of automatic persistence is a hypothesis; the unsaved replacement code path is observed source behavior.

##### Correction

Track persistence status and guard replacement, or persist recoverable active drafts automatically; make persistence failures visible and preserve the active work.

##### Acceptance

Apply a distinctive change, switch worlds, reopen and reload. Confirm the change persists or an explicit save/discard decision preceded departure. With storage failure, no success status appears and active work remains recoverable.

##### Evidence

- E1

- E3

- E5

- E10

### R1-research: Evidence quality and usability research simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-research

#### Seat

research

#### Assessment

Not assessed numerically: product-wide usability, arbitrary-input reliability, cross-age suitability and commercial validation exceed this evidence. Six named real-pipeline records were accepted in 21.8–55.8 seconds; that supports feasibility for those inputs, not a population success rate. Separate current-runtime play notes support completion of recorded Clockwork, cat and kitchen worlds. They do not represent unassisted first-time creation. I inspected the timing files, gamefeel evidence, discovery progress source and pixels for creation, drawing, cat progress, kitchen completion and corrected Dino. The creation and drawing screens have calm hierarchy and clear primary tasks; source artwork remains prominent in the inspected games. Those are positive commercial-craft observations, not proof of purchase intent or learnability. The cat screen visibly says 5/5 found while asking for whiskers; whether users interpret this correctly needs testing. Kitchen completion at 0/5 optional establishes an extremely short permitted route, not enjoyment or a logic failure. No sampled participant ages, independent users, assistance counts, retention or willingness-to-pay measures are present. The corrected Dino appearance supersedes the legacy silhouette evidence; it must not inherit the legacy playthrough as a newly verified completion. A commercially compelling demonstration is plausible, but current evidence supports a bounded demonstration claim rather than a validated general promise.

#### Strengths

- Evidence distinguishes source intent, screenshot appearance, recorded runtime play and real generation timing; corrected Dino provenance is explicitly documented.

- Inspected creation and drawing screens present a clear initial action, and inspected games retain recognizable personal source artwork.

- Recorded play establishes multiple working interaction modes and completion paths, a stronger demonstration basis than static mockups alone.

#### Concerns

- Unknown: no first-time-user end-to-end task-success denominator, assistance measure or age-stratified comprehension evidence exists in the supplied record.

- Unknown: six accepted named inputs do not establish arbitrary-input reliability; selection, failures outside the record, retries and intervention are not characterized here.

- Observed interface state: cat progress shows 5/5 found while a final required whisker action remains; confusion is a testable hypothesis rather than measured user failure.

- Observed via root live QA and inspected before/after artifacts: applying a rename, switching worlds without Save, and reopening loses the edit with no warning. This is a concrete creator-task failure boundary, separate from unknown general success rates.

### R1-gameplay: Game systems, challenge and level design simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R1-gameplay

#### Seat

gameplay

#### Assessment

6.5/10 for mission, agency, challenge, progression and completion in two recorded platform worlds. I completed both using ordinary keyboard input. Dino supplies a readable destination and a small jump sequence; Clockwork adds five collectibles, an upper route and a deliberate descent before its exit unlocks. These are real playable structures. Commercially, however, the inspected experience remains a brief traversal vignette: there is little demonstrated escalation, optional mastery, or consequential choice, and the payoff says little about what the player accomplished.

#### Strengths

- Mission language and bracketed destinations make the next broad action legible. Clockwork’s objective changes from collection to reaching its portal after5/5, giving the sequence a clear second phase.

- Both games actually completed; Clockwork required moving left to a paired orb and dropping through an upper platform, providing more agency than simply holding right. Preserve ordinary keyboard play, approachable recovery intent and creator-facing continuation.

#### Concerns

- Collected source orbs remain luminous in Clockwork; only the surrounding brackets fade. This weakens the distinction between remaining objectives and already completed locations.

- Dino ended after two jump inputs, while Clockwork’s full collection/exit took seven short input bursts. This is useful first-run accessibility but weak evidence of sustained challenge or meaningful progression.

- The identical World complete screen gives neither a world-specific accomplishment recap nor an optional mastery target; Replay from start repeats the same task without a stated new purpose.

#### Evidence

- E1

- E2

- GP1

- GP2

- GP3

- GP4

#### Responses

None recorded.

#### Proposals

##### Id

P-r1-gameplay-1

##### Action

The inspected games offer limited evidence of meaningful choice, evolving challenge, or replay value.

### R2-brand: Distinctiveness and visual identity simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

R2-brand

#### Seat

brand

#### Assessment

I maintain 7/10 for desktop visual identity and commercial craft. The complete first round supports restraint, but it sharpens what a source-led payoff should mean. R1-gamefeel supplies a useful counterexample: the kitchen can reach its notebook with 0/5 optional discoveries. Enlarging the artwork or adding a warmer congratulation would not make that accomplishment deeper. The completion should name the actual achievement, rather than imply mastery or comprehensive discovery. R1-market correctly values Continue creating as the main next action; a brand treatment should preserve that hierarchy. R1-color also makes an important counterargument to reducing the dark overlay: it currently guarantees legibility across unpredictable imagery. I therefore prefer testing a small clear source-detail area with a solid text backing, rather than globally brightening the entire background. This is a comparison proposal, not an established superior design. The generic sparkle is not itself a usability defect. Its limitation is that the same visual message under-recognizes very different creative outcomes. The library clipping remains a directly observed finish defect; QA duplicates do not justify expanding this brand recommendation into a search/archive project.

#### Strengths

- The neutral shell accommodates wildly different art; source identity should remain the distinguishing asset.

- Continue creating expresses the creative-product purpose without requiring an additional reward destination.

#### Concerns

- A more expressive completion must not exaggerate what was achieved or undermine contrast.

- Recognition and emotional payoff remain hypotheses; missing human studies do not prove rejection.

R1-journey identifies the more consequential boundary: a correct editor command is insufficient if its result disappears after “World updated.” Repair that handoff before building a richer history UI. I qualify my R1 role/removal recommendation: identity protection is useful because goals, switches and earned progress reference objects. A required collectible changed to scenery cannot simply retain a collected ID and silently disappear from requirements. Nevertheless, permanent immutability prevents correcting a genuinely wrong generated role. Choose an explicit correction transaction that lists affected requirements/connections and offers a revised-world copy when existing progress cannot be migrated safely. This is a larger capability, not the immediate Delete fix. R1-accessibility’s conditional reset instruction is accurate but insufficient: Delete should not reset several unrelated pending properties. Disable it for protected objects and expose Reset changes separately until reference-aware removal exists. For history, a bounded restoration point for explicit reset/removal is a useful first step; a full branching timeline is unjustified. Selection must remain full-opacity regardless of progress.

### R2-research: Evidence quality and usability research simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

Keep the outcome-level rating unassessed. GP5 now verifies completion of the corrected current Dino fixture; the legacy appearance limitation no longer prevents that narrow claim. Its two input bursts and recorded wall time still do not measure novice difficulty or retention. R1-gameplay and R1-gamefeel legitimately assess the short authored routes, but optional mastery should be tested as a product direction, not prescribed as the proven cure for weak retention. A creator may value sharing one brief personal game rather than replaying it. Conversely, E11 upgrades R1-journey's source-supported persistence concern to a reproduced loss during normal world switching: research is not needed to establish that behavior. R1-library's clipping also merits direct repair, whereas QA duplicates do not establish organic collection pressure or justify archive priority. I narrow my first-round validation proposal into two stages: first repeat the exact loss, Delete and final-step scenarios with deterministic recorded worlds; then run a small diagnostic session with three to five first-time creators covering mode prediction, one personal-input creation, edit/save/reopen and voluntary continuation. Add a separately declared small unseen-input matrix to expose generation failure modes. These bounded checks can guide the next iteration; they cannot establish cross-age universality, input reliability rates or retention. The visible source-led craft remains positive evidence throughout.

- GP5 resolves current Dino playability provenance positively without treating the legacy playthrough as current.

### R3-typography: Typography and readability simulated specialist perspective

#### Id

I maintain the local 14px/21px launch-caption comparison, rather than a global increase to secondary text. It should retain the existing dark backing and bounded measure; recover surrounding whitespace before reducing controls or concealing instructions. - Library metadata clipping remains a direct defect, ahead of optional typography refinement.
